                 Reference is made to an article which appeared in                           ..~-.::{

       inaccurate information regarding FBI inves 'gations, indi-(J<,p                        -·
       eating that the FBI conducts certain inquirl s regarding                             ~.:.,. ..
      flying saucers at the request of the Air Force. It is          ·......_
      pointed out here that, although the Bureau did at one time
      conduct some investigations regarding flying saucers, a
      present agreement has been set up with the Air Force whereby
      the Air Force conducts all investigations pertaining to flying
      saucers and the Bureau, upon receiving complaints of this nature,
      merely turns the complaints over to the Office of Special
      Investigations (OSI), which in turn transmits the information
      to Air Intelligence, Air Intelligence has set up the Air
      Technical Intelligence Center at Wright-Patterson Air Force
      Base, Dayton, Ohio, for the purpose of coordinating and handling
      of research pertaining to Jlying saucers.
                 Inquiry was conducted in order to determine, if
      possible, the source for the information appearing in the
      attached article regarding FBI investigations, Lieutenant
      Colonel L. L. Free in charge of the Espionage Branch,
      Counter-Intelligence Division, Office of Special Investigations,
      advised that no one in OSI has been contacted by        and he
      suggested direct contact by the Bureau Representative with     II
      Air Intelligence to determine if ..... had been in touch with ~p
      anyone in that organization in order to gather information Jar
      his article,

  General John A. Samford, Director of Air Int ell ig ence, ad vi sed

                             DEPARTMENT OF DEFENSE
                               OFFICE OF fiUBLIC INFORMATION
                                      WASHINGTON 2.5, D. C.




     The following 1nforroution conccr.-nine Air Force investigations of unusual
aerial phenomena ie submitted 1n an:Ner to your request.

     In the Fall of 19~7 the ~ted States Air Force took official notice of
reports of eo-called "flying saucers" because the reports from the public
indicated that the problem might be related to the Air Force responsibility for
the a1r defense of .the:! United StateA.

     On December !>0, 19S..7 the AJ.:r Foree d:trccted its Air »J.ter1el COlllm!lnd 1 at
Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, ])Byton, Ohio, to sot up a project t.o collect and
evalue.te all available facte concerning "fly1Il8 saucer" aightings.
     To perform this task the Air M<l.teriel C=and obtained the services of
c1Tilian and military aetron~rs, psychologisto, electronic specialists,
meteorologists, aeronautical engineers, and ~hyeiciete.
     On December 2?, 1949, after !>75 reported llighttnes had been investigated, the
Air Foree, with the concurrence of t.he Arr!rl and the Navy, announced t.he findiuge
of the "flying eaucer" project.
      The evidence at that time indicated that the majority of. the reports of
unidentified flyinefobjects could be accounted for aa miBinterpretaticno of
~ar1oue conventional objecta, a mild form of hysteria, meteorological phenomena,
light. aberrations, or boaxea.                                         ·
     'rhere remained, hovever, a number of unexplained eightings, and the Air
Force has continu.ed it11 1nveet1gat1ons innBlmlch as it is an Air Force res.pona1-
bil1ty to identify and analyze aerial phenomena that could poaaibly be a menace
to the United States.

     Subsequent to December 19~9, these investigations have been conducted au
a normal intelligence fknct1on, rather than a special project, by tho Air
Technical Intelligence Center, Wright-Fattorson Air Force Base, Dayton, Ohio.

     To date, the Air Force has undertal:cn to investigate and an:llyze .about
1500 reports dealing with these phenomena. As beforo, most of the reports were
identified and disposed of as friendly aircraft erroneously reported, known
electronic and meteorological phenonena, light aberrations, h08Xes, and other
known nett~al occ~renccs or can-made obJects,

     The unexplained reports, hovever, which are in the order of 20 percent of
the total, cannot be definitely associated with these fnmiliar things,

                        Difficultv in Evaluating ~orts

     The difficulty in disposing of those unexplained roporte is based largely
upon the insufficiency of acct~ate basic data such as eizo, shape, composition
and flight characteristics (speed, acceleration, altitude, exact maneuver pattern,
etc.) of tho obJects,

     Although some instr~ents which are useful in obtaining more accurate data
of this type have been available, tho reports based on sightincs with these
instrQ~ents have been very infrequent and comprise an extremely omall percentage
of the total, ~breover, e;ven these rep:>rts havE> not included cuch of the
information required.

     Because of tho inadequacy of this basic data, the ft~r Force haS in the past
devoted its efforts pr~marily to determining whether these unexplained sightings
indicated the existence of a menace to the United States. Initially it vas
believed thet some pattern might evolve froc the study of a large voltlllle of
reports. To date, no pattern has materialized to reveal anything whatsoever
which can be interpreted as indicative of purpe>se or consistency or which can be
construed as a menace t:> the United States. Nonetheless, since these unexplained
sightings persist, the Air Force will continue its investigations, giving the
problem adequate but not frantic attention.

     It is nov felt, however, that reports from people whose training and
experience in observine aerial objects qualify them to obtain essential data
are the only ones likely to produce material suitable for systematic analysis.
Tho Air Force is planning to provide additional tools to help these observers
obtain the baste data it needs.

     Repe>rts of similar phenomena go back to Biblical times, There have boen
flurries of them in various centuries, The current series of sightings began
generally in 1946.
                 ~
      Thoro aro ~ reasons why the volume of theae reports hoe increased
 materially during tho past few years. Aerial activity originated by man has
 increased, and pcople·toaay have a greeter curiosity about this activity than
 before. Also, our present officiant communication facilities and nave media
 provide on incentive for reporting unusual observations, for publicizinG them
 and for recording them. Ebvever, the ability to measure these phenocona docs
 not seem to hwe advanced in stop with opportunities for observing them. Tho
_Air Force believes that most of these phenomena will gradually bo understood
 as more 1s known about occurrences in the upper atmosphere.
